# qutip-trap: specification of a first-principles trapped-ion quantum computer simulator

This document specifies the physics, the numerics and the design of the `qutip_trap` package and of its application, `qutip_trap_app`. Everything in it is implemented except what Section 1.3 puts out of scope. Part I (Sections 1-3) sets the scope, the physical process and the architecture. Part II (Section 4) is the physics, one subsection per module, with Section 4.5 the atomic-structure layer that derives every hyperfine-resolved quantity. Part III (Sections 5-8) covers the numerics (the governing equations are collected in Section 5.7), the noise, the control stack and the readout. Part IV holds the validation targets (Section 9), the performance model (11), the open physics (12), the conventions (13), the application (14) and the sources (15). Section numbers are identifiers that the code and the application cite, so they are never renumbered; 4.6, 9.10 to 9.17 and 10 are unassigned.

Provenance tags: **[verified]** checked against the primary source; **[corrected]** the printed or an extracted form was wrong and the form given here is the corrected one; **[extracted]** taken from a primary source with a quote, not independently checked; **[background]** standard physics supplied without a source check; **[recomputed here]** computed by this repository's code and pinned by its tests. A tag records what checking was done, not that a value is final: the tests of Section 9 define correctness, and nothing tagged [background] is relied on without a test that pins it. The provenance ledger `docs/provenance/ledger.yaml` (Section 14.5) carries one record per quantity with the same tags, plus [derived] (computed by the simulator from cited inputs) and [contested] (unresolved between sources).

## 1. Purpose, scope and what "first principles" means here

### 1.1 Goal

`qutip_trap` takes a quantum circuit and returns measurement results the way a trapped-ion quantum processor does, by simulating the physical process that produces them rather than by applying ideal gate matrices with an error model bolted on. The chain it reproduces:

1. A trap potential holds N ions of a chosen species; their equilibrium positions and collective motional modes follow from the trap parameters and the Coulomb interaction.
2. Laser cooling and optical pumping prepare the motional and internal states: a thermal (or near-ground) state per mode and a qubit state with a physical preparation error.
3. The circuit is compiled to the machine's native gates and then to a pulse schedule (which ions each pulse illuminates, with what Rabi-frequency envelope, detuning, phase and duration), every parameter taken from calibrations the simulator performs on the simulated device.
4. Each pulse evolves the joint state of qubits and motional modes under the spin-motion Hamiltonian, with heating, dephasing, photon scattering, addressing crosstalk and laser noise entering as physical channels whose rates follow from the device parameters.
5. Measurement is state-dependent fluorescence: photon counts over a detection window with leakage between the bright and dark manifolds, detector efficiency, background and neighbour crosstalk; a threshold or time-resolved discriminator turns counts into bits.
6. Results are returned per shot (bitstrings and optionally the photon-count records) and aggregated into counts and probabilities in the formats a cloud QPU returns.

### 1.2 What "exactly" can and cannot mean

The simulator is exact at the level of a well-defined physical model and reports the three places where any simulation of this system approximates:

- **Model level.** Each ion is a small set of electronic levels (the qubit pair plus, where it matters, the leakage manifold), each motional mode a quantum harmonic oscillator, and light couples to them through the Hamiltonians of Part II. The optical rotating-wave approximation is taken everywhere (relative error Ω/ω_optical, below 10⁻⁸). Nothing else is dropped by default: the carrier, every sideband, the Debye-Waller factors, the off-resonant terms and the full time dependence of every pulse are kept, because the Hamiltonian is built from the exact displacement operator e^{iη(a+a†)} rather than from its Lamb-Dicke expansion. Raman drives add two approximations of comparable size, both reported with their magnitude: the adiabatic elimination of the far-detuned excited manifold, whose residual excited population is what the scattering channels use, and the replacement of the counter-rotating two-photon pairing at twice the qubit splitting by its second-order shift (Section 4.5.4). The Lamb-Dicke and sideband rotating-wave approximations are options, for speed and for reproducing textbook results, never defaults.
- **Truncation level.** Motional Fock spaces are truncated under the convergence policy of Section 5.5, and every run reports the population that reached the truncation boundary, so a result is either converged or flagged.
- **Size level.** The joint space of N ions and M modes has dimension 2^N Π_m d_m and becomes intractable beyond a few thousand: two or three ions with two or three resolved modes, or more ions with fewer resolved modes and frozen spectators (Sections 5.4, 11). Above that the GATE_LOCAL level (Section 5.4) simulates each pulse exactly on the ions it addresses and every mode they couple to, carries the motional state between gates by a tracked model, and reports the approximation it introduces; Section 9.8 validates it against the joint level where both are feasible.

### 1.3 Non-goals

- Transport: shuttling, splitting, merging and junctions. The simulator models one crystal in one zone; it has no transport input, and transport dynamics and transport heating are out of scope.
- Photonic interconnects, and quantum error correction logic (the simulator runs circuits; a QEC stack is a client of `Machine.run`).
- Compilation optimality: the compiler is correct and produces the native gate set through verified templates (Section 7.7), an arbitrary two-qubit unitary at three entangling gates; gate-count optimization beyond the standard identities is a client concern.
- Mid-circuit measurement: `measure`, `reset` and `recool` are IR operations and a `Schedule` carries their positions, but the scheduler refuses one that precedes a later gate, because the detection back-action of Section 8.5 is not modelled.
- Crystal melting: background-gas collisions are instantaneous events with an outcome distribution (heating kick, reorder, loss, dark ion; Section 6.7), heralded per shot; after a loss or dark-ion event the remaining ions stay on the nominal crystal, which the diagnostics state.
- Planar crystals: a linear chain is required (the crystal solver raises `ZigzagError` beyond the zigzag threshold), and the multi-ion rf (Floquet) frame is not built, so every multi-ion result is in the pseudopotential frame (Sections 4.1.1, 4.1.7).
- Mixed-species dynamics: the crystal layer computes mixed-species positions, modes and per-species Lamb-Dicke parameters (Section 4.1.7) and sympathetic cooling is modelled at the rate level (Section 4.2.5), but no gate on a mixed crystal is scheduled or validated.
- Recalibration in the loop: drift enters through the dynamical samples at the shot clock and an optional servo (Section 7.5), not through repeated calibration.

### 1.4 Users and interfaces

The user writes or imports a circuit, picks a machine (a device model: species, ion count, trap, beam geometry, noise, detector) and asks for results. The public surface is a `Machine` (a `Device`, the roles its beams play, the calibration table and the option objects `Physics`, `Numerics` and `Readout`) with `run`, `submit` (the same run in a worker process behind a `Job`), `compile`, `schedule`, `engine`, `calibrated`, `estimate`, `error_model` and `hash`; a circuit IR (`Circuit`) with the native gates (GPi, GPi2, MS with two phases and an angle, ZZ, virtual RZ) and a compiler from standard gates; importers for OpenQASM 2 and the IonQ circuit JSON and exporters to IonQ's result formats; a Qiskit `BackendV2`; the laboratory of simulated experiments (Rabi, Ramsey, sideband spectroscopy, thermometry, heating rate, MS and parity scans) that calibration uses and users call to reproduce laboratory measurements; and the benchmarks (randomized benchmarking, GHZ fidelity, quantum volume), each with the simulator's own error budget beside it. The methods of `Machine` walk down the five levels the application shows (Section 14.2): the machine (`run`), the circuit (`compile`), the schedule (`schedule`), the dynamics (`engine`) and the physics (the `Device`). The application (Section 14) uses only this public API.

### 1.5 Constraints inherited from the environment

Python 3.13 (pinned by mise), `uv` for environments and dependencies, QuTiP 5.3.x as the simulation engine (5.3.1 with NumPy 2.5 and SciPy 1.18), NumPy and SciPy as the numerical base. Coefficients are NumPy arrays and Python functions, so Cython is optional; no other simulation framework is used. The application uses Flet (flet.dev), which renders through Flutter: one code base gives a desktop window, a browser UI served from the host process, or packaged bundles, with `flet-charts` for the plots. Flet is an optional extra, so the core has no UI dependency. Event handlers may be async and the page offers `run_task` and `run_thread`, so simulations run in worker processes and post progress through the page's pubsub. Flet's static web build runs Python in the browser through Pyodide, three to five times slower than native and without processes: it is the GitHub Pages site, with QuTiP compiled for Pyodide by the Pages workflow (no WebAssembly wheel exists on PyPI) and the app's worker running in-process.

## 2. The physical process the simulator reproduces

This section narrates the pipeline; Part II gives the equations. It follows the physical states the simulated device passes through for one shot of one circuit.

**Before the circuit.** N ions of one species sit in a linear Paul trap. Their equilibrium positions balance the axial confinement against the mutual Coulomb repulsion; their small oscillations decompose into 3N normal modes, N along each axis, each a harmonic oscillator with its own frequency and pattern of ion participation. The modes are the bus every entangling gate uses, so the simulator computes them from the trap parameters rather than taking them as inputs, and records how far each ion participates in each mode (that participation times the recoil scale of the drive is the ion's Lamb-Dicke parameter for the mode).

**Cooling.** Doppler cooling on the cycling transition brings every mode to a thermal state whose mean phonon number is set by the linewidth and the mode frequency. Resolved-sideband or EIT cooling then brings the gate modes close to the ground state; the simulator produces per-mode thermal states with occupations derived from the cooling model and, on request, simulates the cooling dynamics themselves. Optical pumping puts every ion in the qubit's |0⟩ with a small species-specific residual population elsewhere. This is the initial state of the run: internal states nearly pure, modes thermal, no correlations.

**Compilation and calibration.** The circuit is rewritten into the native gate set: single-qubit rotations about equatorial axes (GPi, GPi2 with a phase), the two-qubit entangling gate (MS with two phases and an angle, or ZZ), and Z rotations that cost nothing because they are absorbed into the phases of later pulses. Each native gate becomes a pulse whose parameters (Rabi-frequency envelope, detuning, phase, duration, the modes whose loops it closes) come from calibration tables the simulator builds by running laboratory experiments on the simulated device: Rabi flopping for the π time, sideband spectroscopy for the mode frequencies, gate scans for the amplitude and detuning of maximal entanglement, phase scans to align the entangling axis with the single-qubit frame.

**Gates.** Each pulse illuminates chosen ions (with a small unavoidable fraction of light on their neighbours) and adds a drive term to the Hamiltonian: the ion's transition coupled to the motion through the exact displacement operator. A resonant single-qubit pulse rotates the spin while the motion, with co-propagating beams or a microwave drive, barely notices. A Mølmer-Sørensen pulse drives both sidebands slightly off resonance; the spin-dependent force pushes the driven modes around closed loops in phase space, so that when the loops close the motion is back where it started and the two spins have acquired a joint geometric phase. Throughout, the modes heat at a rate set by electric-field noise, the spins dephase from magnetic-field and laser noise, and the drive scatters photons at a rate set by its detuning and power. The simulator evolves the joint state under all of it, pulse by pulse, idle intervals included.

**Readout.** A detection beam on the cycling transition makes a bright ion scatter photons at a rate set by saturation and detuning and a dark ion scatter almost none, except that off-resonant excitation slowly pumps dark to bright and bright to dark. A fraction of the photons reaches the detector, background arrives too, and some of one ion's light lands on its neighbour's detector. The simulator samples each ion's count record from this model, applies the discriminator the device would, and returns bits. For optical and shelved qubits the dark state is a metastable level whose decay during the window is a further error channel.

**Results.** One shot is one sample of the whole chain; shots aggregate into counts and probabilities. The result also carries the diagnostics that make the run auditable: the truncation-boundary populations, the modes included, the fidelity level, the calibration used and, on request, the final density matrix or trajectory states.

## 3. Architecture

### 3.1 Design principles

- **Physics objects, not gate matrices.** A unitary matrix appears only in the compiler's definition of what a native gate should do and in the validation suite that compares simulated pulses with it; the simulator never applies it to the state.
- **One Hamiltonian builder.** Every pulse (a calibration scan, a single-qubit gate, a Mølmer-Sørensen gate, a cooling pulse) is a set of drive terms on a shared Hilbert space assembled by the same builder. Approximations are options of the builder, defaulting to none.
- **Device parameters in, everything else derived.** The user specifies physical parameters (species, trap frequencies or voltages, beam wavelengths, geometry and powers, magnetic field, noise spectral densities, detector). Lamb-Dicke parameters, Rabi frequencies, Stark shifts, scattering and heating rates, the mode structure and the gate pulse parameters are derived, the last by the simulator's own calibration.
- **Every run reports its own approximation status.** Truncation-boundary populations, mode classes, frames and the fidelity level (Section 5.4) are attached to every result.
- **Deterministic by seed.** Every stochastic element (quantum-jump trajectories, quasi-static noise samples, photon counts) draws from keyed substreams of one root seed, spawned by (sample, trajectory, shot, ion, channel), so reproducibility survives parallel execution and truncation retries (Section 3.4).

### 3.2 Package layout

```
qutip_trap/
  __init__.py      Circuit, Machine, the option objects, FidelityLevel, Result, presets; everything else is
                   imported from its defining module
  machine.py       Machine: device, roles, calibration table, options, level policy (1.4, 3.3)
  options.py       Physics, Numerics and Readout, the option objects of a run
  presets.py       the example machines yb171_chain(n) and ca40_optical(n)
  units.py         CODATA constants, the Landé factor and the unit rules (5.6)
  hashing.py       canonical serialization and digests (3.3)
  provenance.py    provenance tags and the ledger reader (14.5)
  published.py     the closed forms of the published experiments the app's presets compare against (9)
  species/         one table per isotope, every number cited; the angular-momentum layer that derives everything
                   hyperfine-resolved: wigner, zeeman, dipole, polarization, raman, quadrupole, metastable (4.5)
  trap/            Trap record, Mathieu and Floquet, micromotion, pseudopotential, surface electrodes, crystal and
                   modes, anharmonic couplings, heating (4.1)
  light/           beams and beam roles, derived Raman and microwave drives, comb drives, the optical-Bloch
                   scattering-rate object, recoil (4.2.8, 4.3, 4.5.4, 4.5.5)
  control/         native gates, the circuit IR and compiler, KAK, pulses, the scheduler and its virtual-Z
                   frame, the calibration table, pulse shaping, composite pulses, the played and hardware
                   chains (4.3.5, 4.4.3, 7)
  dynamics/        the composite space with the ENR option, displacement operators and truncation, the one
                   Hamiltonian builder and its multi-level mode, collapse operators, solvers, the JOINT_EXACT engine,
                   the exact rotating frame, the factorized kernel, steady states, parallel maps, process
                   tomography (5, 11.3)
  prep/            Doppler, sideband, EIT and polarization-gradient cooling, rate coefficients, optical pumping,
                   the preparation recipe and sequence, validity guards (4.2)
  readout/         fluorescence rates, photon records, discriminators and POVMs, published operating points (8)
  noise/           NoiseModel, spectra, the dynamical sample and its stochastic processes, scattering channels
                   and the leakage levels they fill, collisions, dynamical decoupling and filter functions,
                   circuit-level summaries (6)
  device/          Device and Field, derived quantities with provenance ids, serialization, specs, presets (3.3)
  run/             the pipeline, the stages of a run and its RunRecord, space and mode classes, fidelity levels,
                   GATE_LOCAL, results and diagnostics, RunSpec and Job (3.4, 5.4)
  calibration/     simulated device -> CalibrationTable: calibrate and its cache, the surrogate, the experiments,
                   the entangling spot checks, detection (7.5)
  experiments/     the laboratory: single-ion, motional, light, micromotion, entangling, readout and imaging
                   experiments with typed, fitted results (7.5, 7.9)
  benchmarks/      Clifford groups, randomized benchmarking, GHZ, quantum volume, the budget, the error model (7.9)
  io/              OpenQASM 2 and IonQ JSON (7.6, 8.6)
  interop/         the Qiskit BackendV2
tests/             the test suite (Section 9)
qutip_trap_app/    the application (Section 14): a separate package that imports the core only in core.py
docs/              the API page, the examples and the provenance ledger
```

`control/` never imports `calibration/` (the calibration table is a plain data object the scheduler reads), and the core never imports the application or Flet; tests check both.

### 3.3 Core data model

- `Species`: immutable atomic data for one isotope (mass, nuclear spin and moment, levels with energies, lifetimes, hyperfine constants and measured g_J, transitions with wavelengths, linewidths and the branching the angular algebra does not fix, the qubit pair, the cycling, repump and shelving transitions), every number cited. Everything hyperfine-resolved is derived (Section 4.5), never typed in.
- `Field`: the static magnetic field at the ions; it defines the quantization axis in which polarizations are decomposed and feeds every Zeeman shift.
- `Trap`: explicit secular frequencies with a radial principal-axis angle and the ion mass they are quoted for (`reference_mass_u`, needed by a crystal of several masses), or (V_rf, Ω_rf, U_dc, geometry) converted through the Mathieu module (Section 4.1.1), plus the stray field and the shim voltages that compensate it, from which the residual excess-micromotion index per beam direction follows. The axis angle exists because a mode with no projection on any cooling beam is uncoolable.
- `Crystal`: from the trap, the species list and N: equilibrium positions, the 3N normal modes indexed by position in `Crystal.modes` (Section 13, "Mode index"), and the per-ion per-mode Lamb-Dicke parameters for a given Δk (`Crystal.lamb_dicke`, which applies C₀ once, Section 4.1.1).
- `Beam`: wavelength, k-vector, polarization, waist, power and pointing; the per-ion intensity follows from the beam profile, which is where addressing crosstalk originates. `BeamRoles` names the beams that play each gate, cooling and detection role.
- `Tone`, `Drive`, `Pulse`: a physical drive on a set of ions (Raman, single-photon optical or microwave) with per-tone envelopes, detunings and phases, its effective Δk and Stark shift, derived from the beams (Section 4.3); a pulse is a drive with start and end times, linked to the gate it implements.
- `HilbertSpace`: the composite space with ion qudit dimension d (2 by default, more when leakage is modelled), the resolved modes with their truncation, and cached operators.
- `NoiseModel`: spectral densities and stochastic-process parameters with the derived rates (Section 6).
- `Device`: the aggregate (species, trap, beams, field, noise, detector, roles, preparation recipe); `derived()` returns every computed quantity with the ledger id of its provenance.
- `CalibrationTable`: the pulse parameters the scheduler reads, each entry with its uncertainty, age, status and provenance (Section 7.5).
- `Machine`: a device, its calibration table (the closed-form surrogate of Section 7.5 when none is pinned), the option objects `Physics` (which effects are simulated), `Numerics` (how the integration is done) and `Readout` (how photon records are read), and a `FidelityLevel` (`AUTO`, `JOINT_EXACT`, `GATE_LOCAL`); variants are `dataclasses.replace(machine, ...)`.
- `Circuit`: operations on qubit indices in a small IR (native and standard gates, measure, reset, recool). `Schedule`: pulses and events with absolute times, and the per-qubit phase frame at each point. `Result`: per-shot records, aggregates and the `diagnostics` block.

**Identity.** Every object is immutable after construction, and frequencies in the public API are in Hz, converted to rad/s at the boundary (Section 5.6). Identity for the calibration cache and the run record is `Device.hash()` (and `Machine.hash()`), a canonical serialization rather than Python's `hash()`, which `frozen=True` does not supply for ndarray, dict and `Qobj` fields: dataclass fields in declaration order, floats rounded to 12 significant digits, ndarrays as dtype, shape and C-order bytes, dicts by sorted key, callables by qualified name plus a digest of their source, `Qobj` fields excluded, and fields marked `metadata={"hash": "exclude"}` left out. `Device.roles` is such a field (it is the operator's assignment of beams, not the apparatus), so `Machine.hash()` carries the roles. The same device yields the same digest in every process.

### 3.4 Control flow of `run`

```
Machine + Circuit
  -> compile: Circuit -> native gates, phase-tracked                   [control.compiler]
  -> calibrate (cached per device): CalibrationTable                   [calibration, through the engine]
  -> schedule: native gates -> pulses and events with absolute times   [control.schedule]
  -> prepare: motional state per mode, internal state per ion          [prep]
  -> evolve: per pulse, build H(t) and the collapse operators; advance [dynamics]
       (idle intervals: free evolution with heating and dephasing)
  -> readout: fluorescence -> photon counts -> bits                     [readout]
  -> Result
```

Shots come from the ensemble structure of the physics, with one vocabulary throughout: a **sample** is one draw of every quasi-static parameter (Section 6.1); a **trajectory** is one quantum-jump unravelling within a sample, or the single deterministic evolution of a pure branch or a density matrix; a **shot** is one measurement record. `Machine.run(circuit, shots)` deals the shots to S samples (`Numerics.samples`) in contiguous blocks of the shot clock (sample k owns M_k = shots // S shots, plus one for the first shots mod S samples, and is drawn at t₀ + Σ_{j<k} M_j T_rep; `Result.sample_of_shot` maps them back), with S = min(shots, 64) by default when the noise model has quasi-static or sampled content and one nominal sample when it is quiet **[corrected]**. One quasi-static draw per contiguous block is what the hardware does, and a round-robin allocation would give every sample the same mean shot time and no between-sample spread under drift. The trajectory count per sample is the fixed `ntraj` with a keyed seed list; optionally (`Numerics.trajectory_target_tol`) a first `mcsolve` phase under a serial map estimates it from `target_tol` on the population observables, and a second phase replays a keyed seed list of that length, because `target_tol` stops at a point that depends on scheduling. The diagnostics report the realized (samples, trajectories, shots per sample) and the effective sample size behind the error bars, since shots drawn from one sample are not independent draws from the ensemble: with per-sample frequencies p_s the pooled estimate is p̂ = Σ M_s p_s / Σ M_s, Var(p̂) is the shot-weighted variance of the p_s over S − 1 (it already contains the within-sample binomial term), and n_eff = p(1 − p)/Var(p̂), the minimum over the bitstrings seen, capped at the shot count **[corrected]**. Estimators are chosen per quantity: deterministic `mesolve` or pure-branch `sesolve` where the joint dimension allows it (Section 5.3), first-order perturbative channel evaluation for error rates at the 10⁻⁵ to 10⁻⁶ level that no trajectory count could resolve, trajectories otherwise, and Gauss-Hermite quadrature rather than Monte Carlo over quasi-static parameters when a closed form is reproduced.

Randomness comes from one root `SeedSequence` per run, spawned by (sample, trajectory, shot, ion, channel); the shot slot exists because photon counts are sampled per shot per ion. Every variate's key is therefore independent of execution order, of the worker count and of truncation retries, and the engine averages trajectories itself in keyed order, so a run over 1 and over 18 workers agrees shot by shot and to 10⁻¹² in the register (QuTiP's `MultiTrajResult` sums in completion order, 4.4 × 10⁻¹⁶ apart, and is not used for the average). The same (`shots`, `samples`, `seed`, machine hash) reproduces every shot, whether the run went through `Machine.run` or a `Job` in a worker process. A different `shots` does not reproduce the shots two runs share, because the block sizes and the default sample count move with it and the per-shot draws are keyed by the sample and the shot index inside its block. Integrator and truncation changes may move sampled outcomes, since trajectory and readout draws follow the state they are drawn from, so records are compared by their histograms within the error bars and by their diagnostics, never shot list against shot list.

# Part II. Physics specification

## 4. Physical models

### 4.1 Trap potential, ion crystal, motional modes, micromotion and heating

#### 4.1.1 Potential and Mathieu equation

Near the trap centre the potential is quadrupolar in both its static and its rf part (Leibfried, Blatt, Monroe, Wineland, RMP 75, 281 (2003), Eqs. 1-2) **[verified]**:

Φ(x, y, z, t) = ½ U (α x² + β y² + γ z²) + Ũ cos(ω_rf t) · ½ (α′ x² + β′ y² + γ′ z²),  α + β + γ = α′ + β′ + γ′ = 0.

Each coordinate obeys a Mathieu equation, in the RMP 2003 convention **[verified]**:

d²x/dξ² + [a_x − 2 q_x cos(2ξ)] x = 0,  ξ = ω_rf t / 2,  a_x = 4 Z|e| U α / (m ω_rf²),  q_x = 2 Z|e| Ũ α′ / (m ω_rf²),

with q_y = −q_x in a linear trap. Wineland 1998 and Berkeland 1998 write +2q cos, a half-rf-period time shift that flips the phase of micromotion relative to the rf drive and changes nothing else **[verified]**. β is even in q, so only a_x ≠ a_y splits the two radial frequencies **[corrected]**. Rod and blade traps use Berkeland's geometry, a_x = a_y = −a_z/2 = −4QκU₀/(mZ₀²Ω²), q_x = −q_y = 2QV₀/(mR′²Ω²) with R′ ≅ R **[corrected]**, taking κ, R′ and Z₀ as inputs because no source gives them for a real design; surface traps take their curvatures from Section 4.1.6 or from an external field solution through a = 4QΦ″_dc/(mΩ²), q = 2QΦ″_rf/(mΩ²) (Brownnutt 2015) **[verified]**.

**Exponent and stability.** To lowest order β ≈ (a + q²/2)^{1/2} and ν = βω_rf/2 **[verified]**; the preprint form [(a + q²/2)/(1 − 3q²/8)]^{1/2} is 0.12% from the exact β at q = 0.3, against 1.8% for the lowest-order form **[corrected]**. The exact β comes from the monodromy matrix M of one period in ξ: stable when |tr M| < 2, cos(πβ) = tr M/2 on the branch 0 ≤ β ≤ 1 (the edge at a = 0 is q = 0.908), and the 6 × 6 vector monodromy when the static and rf curvature matrices do not commute **[background]**. RMP 2003 Eqs. (10)-(11) carry an off-by-one index error; the recursion is D_{2n} = [a − (2n + β)²]/q **[corrected]**. The secular phase per rf period is πβ; the RMP prints βω_rf T, twice this **[corrected]**.

**Pseudopotential.** At a ≈ 0 the radial frequency is ω_r = QV₀/(√2 Ω_T m R²) = qΩ_T/(2√2) (Wineland 1998 Eq. 6, whose q is the charge; the charge Q and the Mathieu q are kept apart) **[corrected]**. Secular frequencies are computed from (a, q), with voltage converters for the documented geometries.

**Intrinsic micromotion.** The secular motion is multiplied by a modulation of fractional amplitude q/2 at Ω_T (Wineland 1998 Eq. 4); axial motion in a linear trap has none **[verified]**. In the quantum treatment (RMP 2003 Eqs. 16-29, 37) x̂(t) = x₀[â u*(t) + â† u(t)] with a c-number Floquet function u(t) ∝ e^{iνt}[1 − (q/2) cos(ω_rf t)] to lowest order in the adopted sign (the RMP's and Wineland's [1 + (q/2) cos] is the same solution half an rf period later), and the Floquet recursion gives c_{±1}/c₀ = −q/((2 ± β)² − a) **[verified]**. The in-phase micromotion at the rf phase origin is therefore a contraction, x_μ(t) = −(q/2) x_sec(t) cos(ω_rf t); every micromotion-sideband phase and the sign with which a dc shim adds to Berkeland's φ_ac term follow from it, and the modulation index of Section 4.3.6 steps by π as a shim crosses the compensated value **[corrected]**. Sidebands appear at δ = l′ω_rf + lν with l ≠ 0 if l′ ≠ 0, so an ion at the rf null has no first-order micromotion sideband of the carrier **[corrected]**. The RMP's ν is an angular frequency throughout.

**Micromotion correction C₀.** With x₀ = √(ħ/2mν) and [â, â†] = 1, the canonical commutator fixes the exact Floquet function's normalization through its Wronskian, Im(u* u̇) = ν, not through u(0) = 1. The e^{iνt} Fourier component of u, which the secular sideband couples to, is then C₀ = [Σ_n c_n²(1 + nω_rf/ν)]^{−1/2} = 1 + 3q²/16 + O(q⁴): even in q and slightly above one (1.007741 at q = 0.2) **[corrected]**. The RMP's (1 + q/2)⁻¹ is the u(0) = 1 normalization at one rf phase and depends on the rf clock; it is not used. C₀ comes from the Floquet recursion at the monodromy β and multiplies η exactly once, in `Crystal.lamb_dicke`, which records it in η's provenance; the drive builder (Section 4.3.6) and the Hamiltonian (Section 5.7) never reapply it. It shifts η by 1.8% at q = 0.3 (3.7% in the Mølmer-Sørensen phase), which a sideband calibration (Section 7.5) absorbs and a carrier calibration does not.

**Excess micromotion** (Berkeland et al. 1998) **[verified]**. A uniform stray field displaces the ion by u₀ = QE_dc/(mω²) along each principal axis, with the pseudopotential spring mω² = m(Ω_rf/2)²(a + q²/2), the driven equation's dc Fourier row and Berkeland's own ω **[derived]**. The excess micromotion is the signed peak amplitude u₁ = −(q/2)u₀, and the index for a drive of full wavevector Δk is the signed β = Δk·u₁, so sign(β) = −sign(qE). An rf phase difference φ_ac between electrodes adds the out-of-phase amplitude (1/4)qRαφ_ac, which no shim can null; the in-phase and out-of-phase indices are kept apart and reach the drive as their quadrature sum with the rf phase offset −atan2(β_op, β_ip). Amplitudes are declared peak values (Blakestad 2011 mixes peak and rms in one table) **[corrected]**. In the ion frame the laser phase is modulated as β cos(Ωt + δ) (Berkeland's Eq. 21 prints a stray factor Ω **[corrected]**), so the drive splits into a comb weighted by J_n(β): carrier amplitude J₀(β), first sideband over carrier J₁²/J₀² ≈ (β/2)² **[verified]**. The drive side is Section 4.3.6; the second-order Doppler shift −⟨v²⟩/(2c²) is reported. Shim voltages become a field only on the surface-electrode path (Section 4.1.6); the explicit-frequency and rod/blade paths refuse non-zero shims, since no source gives their field response.

**Explicit rf dynamics.** The pseudopotential expansion does not diagonalize the multi-ion rf problem: each ion's micromotion is locked to its displacement from the rf null, B₂ = −(q/4)B₀ (Landa et al. 2012) **[verified]**. The simulator works in the pseudopotential frame with C₀. What it adds of the rf is the drive-side modulation of excess micromotion (Section 4.3.6): the J₀(β) carrier average by default, and the rf-locked e^{iβ cos(Ω_rf t + δ)} with `micromotion="modulated"`. Landa's Floquet-Lyapunov construction of the time-dependent modes is not implemented (Section 12), so for non-commuting curvature matrices C₀ per axis is a pseudopotential-frame approximation, exact when they commute.

#### 4.1.2 Equilibrium positions of the chain

For N ions of equal charge in a harmonic axial well, held collinear by the transverse confinement (James, Appl. Phys. B 66, 181 (1998), Eqs. 2.1-2.9) **[verified]**, V = Σ_m ½Mν²x_m² + Σ_{m≠n} (Z²e²/8πε₀)/|x_n − x_m|; with ℓ = (Z²e²/(4πε₀Mν²))^{1/3} and u_m = x_m/ℓ,

u_m − Σ_{n<m} 1/(u_m − u_n)² + Σ_{n>m} 1/(u_m − u_n)² = 0,

with u = ∓(1/2)^{2/3} for N = 2 and ∓(5/4)^{1/3}, 0 for N = 3. The crystal module minimizes the full three-dimensional energy, with per-ion springs κ_{i,a} = m_iω_{i,a}² along the principal axes and a uniform stray field, by damped Newton iteration from the James chain, and accepts only a positive-definite Hessian: a buckled chain raises `ZigzagError`, because zigzag and planar crystals are not modelled. The threshold is Marquet's exact α_crit = 2/(μ_N − 1) (Section 4.1.3; (ω_r/ω_z)_crit = 1 and √(12/5) for N = 2 and 3) **[verified]**; the fit 0.73 N^{0.86} is 13 to 15% conservative for N ≥ 10 and 32% high at N = 2, and is not used **[corrected]**. For an infinite chain of spacing s_c the zigzag frequency is ω_r² = (7/(8πε₀)) ζ(3) Q²/(m s_c³), Q the ion charge **[verified]**.

#### 4.1.3 Normal modes

**Axial.** The dimensionless Hessian is A_mm = 1 + 2 Σ_{p≠m} |u_m − u_p|⁻³, A_mn = −2|u_m − u_n|⁻³ (James Eq. 3.3; Marquet, Schmidt-Kaler, James 2003 Eq. 2.9), with orthonormal eigenvectors b^{(p)}, increasing eigenvalues μ_p and ν_p = √μ_p ν; for every N, μ₁ = 1 (centre of mass, b ∝ (1, …, 1)) and μ₂ = 3 (stretch, b ∝ u) **[verified]**. James's Table 2 is sign-consistent with the closed forms **[corrected]**.

**Transverse.** B = (1/α + ½)𝟙 − ½A with α = (ω_z/ω_r)² has A's eigenvectors and eigenvalues γ_p = 1/α + ½ − μ_p/2 **[verified]**. A linear trap has two transverse families, with α_x and α_y along the principal axes (rotated about z by `Trap.axis_angle_rad`), identical eigenvectors and generally different frequencies ω_z(γ_p^{(x,y)})^{1/2}: 3N modes in three families, which a collinear chain solves as three N × N problems so that a degenerate radial pair cannot mix **[corrected]**. Within a family the transverse order is reversed, centre of mass highest and zigzag mode lowest, and the chain buckles at γ_N → 0, α_crit = 2/(μ_N − 1) **[verified]**.

**Mode index and gauge.** A mode index is a position in `Crystal.modes`, ordered axial, transverse_1, transverse_2, ascending in frequency within a family; eigenvectors are unit-norm and mass-weighted with the last component positive (Marquet's gauge) **[corrected]**.

**Quantization.** x̂_k = x_{0,k}(a_k + a_k†), x_{0,k} = (ħ/(2mω_k))^{1/2}, H_mot = Σ_k ħω_k a_k†a_k with the zero-point energy dropped; James's Kittel form with i(a − a†) and ħω/2 per mode is never mixed in **[verified]**. The Lamb-Dicke parameter is η_{j,k} = b_j^{(k)}(Δk·ê_k)(ħ/(2mω_k))^{1/2} (Wineland 1998 Eq. 33; James Eqs. 3.18-3.19, whose coupling constants are s_m^{(p)} = √N b_m^{(p)} μ_p^{−1/4}) **[verified]**; the mixed-species form is Section 4.1.7.

#### 4.1.4 Anharmonic corrections (optional module)

Expanding the Coulomb energy to third and fourth order in the 3N displacements, with its exact derivatives, gives the mode couplings H_anh/ħ = Σ_{k≤l≤m} g_{klm} X_kX_lX_m + Σ_{k≤l≤m≤n} g_{klmn} X_kX_lX_mX_n, X = a + a†, each sorted index tuple carrying its permutation multiplicity; the harmonic trap contributes nothing beyond second order **[background]**. The electrode potential's own anharmonicity is the separate H_curv; both are carried by the opt-in `Trap.anharmonic_terms` record (Section 5.7). For an equal-mass chain (Marquet et al. 2003, Eqs. 2.11-3.5) **[verified]**, C_mnp = (1/6)∂³F/∂u_m∂u_n∂u_p of F = Σ_{m<n} |u_m − u_n|⁻¹ obeys Σ_p C_mnp = 0, so the centre-of-mass mode decouples from all three-mode mixing (D_mn1 = 0), and Σ_p u_pC_mnp = ½(δ_mn − A_mn), so D_mn2 = (1 − μ_m)δ_mn/(2|u|); the axial coefficient of X_pX_qX_r is 2εω_z D_pqr (μ_pμ_qμ_r)^{−1/4} times the multiplicity, with ε = x₀/(4ℓ), x₀ = (ħ/(2Mω_z))^{1/2} (g = εω_z = 2π × 1419 Hz for 40Ca+ at 2 MHz). The cubic energy along the stretch mode is D_222 s³ with D_222 = −1.1225 at N = 2 (the Coulomb spring softens as the pair expands), so with C defined as above the cubic potential is +Σ D_pqr ξ_pξ_qξ_r, with no leading minus sign **[corrected]**. Mixed crystals use the general Cartesian route.

The cubic term has no diagonal Fock elements, so away from a three-mode resonance its effect is second order, a dispersive shift of order g²/Δ_res with Δ_res = |ω_m ± ω_n − ω_p| **[corrected]**. The estimate is the directly integrated two-mode phase: at g/2π = 1.419 kHz over 100 µs, 0.003 to 0.004 rad for mismatches from 30 kHz to 1 MHz and 0.016 rad on the resonant pair |2, 0⟩ ↔ |0, 1⟩ at 100 kHz **[recomputed here]**; g·t is not a phase, and g²t/Δ_res misses the integrated value by a factor 3 low to 13 high across that range. Every build with anharmonic terms runs the resonance checker (mode triples with |ω_p ± ω_q − ω_r| inside the coupling width, the direct condition rather than Marquet's Eq. 4.10) and this estimate; the cubic Hamiltonian itself is opt-in, because it joins all three mode families into one space. Wineland 1998 Eqs. 76-78 add the static-field anharmonicity: frequency renormalization, and resonant three-mode coupling where |ω_l ± ω_m| = ω_k **[verified]**; the printed Eq. 78 must read D_k^i q_i q_m **[corrected]**.

#### 4.1.5 Motional heating and motional decoherence

**Master equation** **[corrected]**:

ρ̇ = −i(ω_t + δ)[a†a, ρ] + (Γ/2)(N̄ + 1)(2aρa† − a†aρ − ρa†a) + (Γ/2) N̄ (2a†ρa − aa†ρ − ρaa†),

with the noise-induced shift δ folded into the calibrated mode frequency. The journal version of Wineland 1998 Eq. 62 prints 2aρ†a† (both arXiv versions are right), and Brownnutt 2015 Eq. 14 gives both terms the decay structure (its Eqs. 18 and 24 are consistent with the form above). A quoted heating rate is d⟨n⟩/dt at n = 0, Γ_h = ΓN̄, not the thermal diffusion rate Γ(2N̄ + 1); for field noise N̄ ≫ n̄, so ṅ ≈ Γ_h independent of n̄ **[verified]**. The collapse operators √(Γ(N̄ + 1)) a and √(ΓN̄) a† are both √Γ_h in that limit; Trout 2018's equal-rate pair is that limit and is never combined with a finite N̄. The coherence of (|n₀⟩ + |m₀⟩)/√2 decays at Γ_h(n₀ + m₀ + 1), the +1 being absorption acting on the lower level **[corrected]**.

**Rate from field noise.** Γ_h = e²S_E(ω)/(4mħω) with the single-sided S_E(ω) = 2∫dτ ⟨δE(τ)δE(0)⟩ e^{−iωτ} (Brownnutt Eqs. 11-12) **[verified]**; the arXiv preprint of Turchette 2000 prints its Eq. (3) as q²S_E/(mħω), which does not follow from its Eq. (2), while the published q²S_E/(4mħω) agrees with Brownnutt **[corrected]**. Spectra are stored two-sided (Section 6.1), and the heating layer evaluates S_E = 2S^{(2)}(|ω|) with the white level counted once. With the rf drive a single ion's rate sums over micromotion sidebands with the Floquet weights of Section 4.1.1, Γ_h = (e²/(4mħω)) Σ_j |c_j/c₀|² S_E(|ω + jΩ_rf|) (Brownnutt) **[verified]**; Turchette's Eq. (4) term (ω²/(2Ω_T²))S_E(Ω_T ± ω) is the same j = ±1 weight at lowest order, a check and never an addend. A run's per-mode rates evaluate S_E at the mode frequency. The rf-voltage-noise heating of an ion displaced from the null (Brownnutt Eq. 13) needs the stray field's rf gradient, which the trap record does not carry, so it is not part of a run's noise model (Section 6.2).

**Multi-ion.** Mode k heats at ṅ_k = (e²/(4ħω_k)) Σ_a Σ_ij c_{ia}c_{ja} g_ij S_E(ω_k)/√(m_im_j) with the mass-weighted components c and the field correlation g_ij = exp(−|r_i − r_j|/ℓ_c) (Brownnutt Eqs. 19-23; Kielpinski 2000 Eq. 20) **[verified]**. Uniform noise (ℓ_c → ∞) heats only the centre-of-mass modes of an equal-mass chain, at N times the single-ion rate (Lechner 2016: 65(7) quanta/s on nine ions against 7.2 for one) **[verified]**, and every mode of a mixed crystal with Σ_j c_j/√m_j ≠ 0 (Section 4.1.7); uncorrelated noise (ℓ_c = 0) heats every mode at the single-ion rate. Transverse gate modes alternate in sign, exactly where uniform noise cancels, so ℓ_c is a device parameter that defaults to 0 and never to the uniform limit **[corrected]**. A measured rate on one mode is inverted to the density it implies, S_E = 4mħωṅ/e² (`trap.heating.s_e_from_heating_rate`).

**Empirical S_E.** S_E ∝ ω^{−α} d^{−β} T^{γ} (Brownnutt Eq. 30) has no a-priori justification; a quoted exponent may be S_E's or ṅ's (ṅ ∝ ω^{−(α+1)}), and α changes under surface treatment **[corrected]**. Measured spans are 10⁻¹⁶ to 10⁻⁶ V² m⁻² Hz⁻¹, β = 2.6-4.0 and α = 0.57-1.5, with medians 40 × 10⁻¹² at 300 K and 0.2 × 10⁻¹² at 6 K for 30-230 µm traps **[verified]**; Johnson noise gives S_E = 4k_BTR/d² and the patch model S_E = 3CS_Vr_p²/(4a⁴) **[verified]**. No first-principles model exists: S_E is a device input (constant, power law or table) with its provenance, and every heating number is apparatus-local; for a surface trap d is the computed ion height (Section 4.1.6).

**Motional dephasing.** Trap-frequency noise is phase damping in a†a (RMP 2003 Eq. 163) **[verified]**: a Lindblad term for white noise and a mode frequency drawn per dynamical sample for slow drift (Section 6.2).

**Measuring n̄ and ṅ.** The thermal sideband ratio I_k^{rsb}/I_k^{bsb} = [n̄/(1 + n̄)]^k and the slope of n̄ against an inserted delay (Turchette Eqs. 8-11) **[verified]** are the thermometry and heating-rate experiments (Section 4.2.7).

#### 4.1.6 Surface-electrode traps from electrode geometry

A planar trap is given by its electrode outlines and voltages in the gapless-plane approximation (House 2008; Wesenberg 2008): coplanar electrodes, zero-width gaps, the plane outside grounded **[verified]**. Coordinates: the substrate is y = 0, y the height, z the trap axis along the rf rails. Electrodes are infinite strips along z or finite rectangles, with widths measured from gap centre to gap centre, which is accurate to second order in the gap width; finite gaps, electrode thickness and arbitrary polygons are not modelled, and an externally solved traceless dc curvature can be added instead.

**Potentials.** Strips use f(w) = (i/π) Σ_k σ_kV_k ln(w − e_k), w = x + iy, σ = +1 at a left edge and −1 at a right edge, with φ = Re f and ∂_x^m∂_y^nφ = Re[iⁿf^{(m+n)}] (Wesenberg 2008) **[verified]**. A rectangle at unit voltage gives (House 2008)

φ = (1/2π) Σ_{i,j=1,2} (−1)^{i+j} arctan[ (x_i − x)(z_j − z) / (y R_ij) ],  R_ij = [(x_i − x)² + (z_j − z)² + y²]^{1/2},

with the principal arctan (never atan2) and ordered corners (one swap negates it); it is odd in y, and its Hessian is the complex-step derivative of the analytic gradient **[recomputed here]**. The unit-voltage basis Θ_k = φ_k/V sums to one on the plane.

**Pseudopotential and the Mathieu map.** Ψ = Q²|E_rf|²/(4mΩ_rf²) with V_rf the peak of a cos(Ω_rf t) drive on both rails in phase: an rms amplitude read as a peak doubles every depth and inflates every rf-set frequency by √2, and Chiaverini's and Madsen's V₀ are differential amplitudes, ±V₀/2 **[corrected]**. The ion sits at the minimum of the total potential, not at the rf null, and the residual rf field there is the excess micromotion of Section 4.1.1. The Hessian there gives a_i = 4QΦ″_dc,i/(mΩ²) and q_i = 2QΦ″_rf,i/(mΩ²); House's traceless matrices A and Q go to the vector monodromy when they do not commute, and both the pseudopotential and the Mathieu frequencies are reported. At a two-dimensional rf null MᵀM = λ²𝟙, so the pseudopotential is isotropic in the transverse plane and the transverse axes are set by the dc potential alone: parallel and perpendicular to the substrate for a symmetric five-wire trap (a beam parallel to the substrate misses the vertical mode), rotated when asymmetric rails move the null (Chiaverini 2005's 30°), near ±45° for four wires **[verified]**. House's Eq. 4 has Q = −q, and his Eq. 7 prints the lower-left block of F(τ) with the wrong sign, which his check KF + FᵀK = 0 misses and which reports a stable trap (a_z = 0.005, q = 0.20) as unstable **[corrected]**.

**Two rf rails** (House 2008 Eqs. 24-29; Nizamani and Hensinger 2012) **[verified]**. With the rails at −c < x < 0 and a < x < a + b the null is at x₀ = ac/(b + c), y₀ = [abc(a + b + c)]^{1/2}/(b + c), moving toward the narrower rail; Nizamani's x₀ = ac/(b + c) sits under a figure with House's rail labels mirrored and reads ab/(b + c) in their own labelling, so the module stores edges rather than widths **[corrected]**. Five wires (c = b) give h = [a(a + 2b)]^{1/2}/2, four wires y₀ = a. The symmetric depth is the pseudopotential at x_E = a/2, y_E = [2ab + a² + 2(a + b)(2ab + a²)^{1/2}]^{1/2}/2,

ψ_E = (Z²e²V_rf²/(π²mΩ²)) [b/((a + b)² + (a + b)(2ab + a²)^{1/2})]²,

maximal at b/a = 1.1914879 for fixed a; House's gapped escape-point radical prints ½ where 2 belongs (31% low) **[corrected]**. The escape point is a saddle: for strips a zero of f″ (a polynomial of degree 2(K − 1) in the K edges), for rectangles found numerically and accepted with exactly one negative Hessian eigenvalue; a search along x = x₀ overestimates the depth for b ≠ c, and House's formula holds for equal rails only. Nizamani's κ equals House's form for c = b, and its fixed-height optima are b/a = 3.676 (h = 1.445a; printed 1.43a) for c = b and 4.902 (h = 1.362a; printed 1.27a) for c = b/2 **[corrected]**. The fixed-height maximum κ_max = (5√5 − 11)/8 = 0.0225425 is geometry independent: House's four-wire coefficient, Wesenberg's optimal-quadrupole bound (5√5 − 11)/(2π²) U₀ with U₀ = Q²V_rf²/(4MΩ²d²), and the numerical maximum for every rail ratio **[recomputed here]**. Nizamani's q = 2eV_rf/(mΩ²h²) is not the Mathieu q (it exceeds (Q₁₁² + Q₁₂²)^{1/2} by 3.2002 for his trap) and never enters a frequency or a stability test; his Eq. 6's 2e on the quartic axial term is a factor 2 against his Eq. 7 **[corrected]**. House's Sec. V frequency of 11.5 MHz drops a 1/(2√2) **[corrected]**.

**dc voltages and reports.** The dc set for a target axial curvature is the minimum-norm solution of House's Eq. 30: zero field at the null and the curvature fixed, which needs at least four electrodes **[verified]**. Every surface device reports the ion height h, which is the d of S_E ∝ d^{−β}, and the trap depth.

#### 4.1.7 Mixed-species crystals and the quantized time-dependent frame

**Single-ion frequencies and modes.** For equal charges the equilibrium is mass independent; at fixed voltages a species' single-ion frequencies follow ω_i²(m) = P_i/m² + S_i/m, the rf and static parts (Home 2013 Eq. 6); the module inverts the exact monodromy at the trap's own Ω_rf and refuses a species the pseudopotential does not confine **[verified]**. On the explicit path the trap names the ion mass its frequencies are quoted for (`Trap.reference_mass_u`), and every ion's Mathieu record, C₀ and micromotion amplitude are that mass's (a, q) times m_ref/m_i: an 88Sr+-quoted trap reproduces the rod-trap voltage map's 40Ca+ record to 10⁻⁹, and a 40Ca+ neighbour in a (3.0, 2.9, 1.0) MHz 88Sr+ trap at 60 MHz carries C₀ = 1.019948 on η, not 88Sr+'s 1.003867. A crystal of several masses in a trap that names none is refused, by the solver and by the `Device` **[derived]**. A uniform stray field shifts every ion by the crystal's linear response K⁻¹eE (`Crystal.field_displacement_m`): rigidly along the axis, where the dc curvature is mass-independent, and coupled by the Coulomb interaction across unequal transverse springs **[derived]**. The modes solve K b = ω² M b as M^{−1/2}KM^{−1/2} c = ω² c with orthonormal mass-weighted c = M^{1/2}b, and

η_{i,k} = c_i^{(k)} (Δk·ê_k) [ħ/(2 m_i ω_k)]^{1/2},

with the ion's own mass under the root (Home 2013 Eqs. 7-19) **[verified]**; for equal masses c is the b of Section 4.1.3. Sørensen and Mølmer's and Leung's per-ion COM η already contains 1/√N, and Srinivas puts the total two-ion mass inside r₀ **[corrected]**. Home's printed slips **[corrected]**: Eq. 13's momentum sign (a_α† − a_α is right, as his Eq. 12 requires), the rf drive counted twice in Eq. 4, the 1/√2 dropped in Eq. 14, complex-conjugate phases in Eq. 21 (red and blue sidebands swap), Ω for Ω/2 in Eq. 24, √m_i inside a sum over j in Eq. 27, η indexed by the wrong ion in Eq. 28, an energy not divided by ħω_α in Eq. 29, and the Mg+ x and y frequencies transposed in the Table I caption.

**Eigenvectors.** Every source's eigenvectors are orthonormal in u_i = √m_i z_i, except Morigi and Walther's oblique Eq. 9 (β′_i = √(m_i/m) q_i); renormalizing raw displacements is a 20 to 160% error that vanishes at μ = 1 **[corrected]**. Mode order is not stable in μ, and "in-phase is lowest" holds axially but not radially (Wübbena 2012 Eqs. 15-16), so modes are sorted by value and identified by structure; an overall sign is the gauge of Section 4.1.3, relative signs are physical, and published η tables are compared up to a per-mode sign and a relabelling **[verified]**. The closed-form checks are the two-ion axial form ω±² = ω_z1²[1 + 1/μ ± (1 − 1/μ + 1/μ²)^{1/2}], μ = m₂/m₁, and Kielpinski 2000's three-ion forms with the impurity at the centre, ζ_{1,3} = [13/10 + (21 ∓ (441 − 34μ + 169μ²)^{1/2})/(10μ)]^{1/2}, ζ₂ = √3, evaluated with isotope masses **[recomputed here]**.

**Cooling and heating.** In the Lamb-Dicke, low-saturation limit participation multiplies a mode's cooling rate by W_k = Σ_{j∈cooled} η_{j,k}² and leaves its steady state unchanged while W_k ≠ 0; a mode dark to the coolant is not cooled (Morigi and Walther 2001 Eqs. 15-17) **[verified]**, and their W_k^max = ω_R/Ω_k holds for a single species only **[corrected]**. A mixed crystal has no exact centre-of-mass mode: every mode with Σ_j c_j^{(k)}/√m_j ≠ 0 heats under uniform noise at ṅ_k = e²S_E(ω_k)/(4ħω_k) (Σ_j c_j^{(k)}/√m_j)² (Kielpinski 2000 Eq. 20). In a reflection-symmetric array the modes with antisymmetric patterns (p = +1 in Morigi and Walther's convention, the central ion at rest) are exempt from uniform noise and cannot be cooled by a centre coolant; the in-phase lowest mode is odd, so "even" is not "in-phase" **[corrected]**. For mass ratios below 0.25 or above 4 the radial modes nearly decouple (Wübbena 2012), a rule of thumb that depends on ε = ω_p/ω_z **[corrected]**. A mode with a small coolant amplitude reaches the Doppler limit more slowly, not a higher one; external heating is what leaves it hot. In a mixed radial mode the linearized Coulomb spring rescales a by 1 + (1 − β)/(2α), β the two ions' amplitude ratio (Wübbena Eqs. B5-B9, in the +2q cos sign) **[verified]**.

**The rf-modulated Coulomb coupling.** Each ion's micromotion is −(q/2) times its radial displacement (Section 4.1.1), so the Coulomb coupling is rf-modulated wherever ions sit off the null (Landa et al. 2012; Kaufmann et al. 2012) **[verified]**: a linear chain on the null has exact pseudopotential modes to lowest order, while zigzag crystals, displaced chains and mixed radial modes shift (Kaufmann's three-ion 40Ca+ zigzag modes by 2 to 3% at q ≈ 0.2). The quantized multi-ion Floquet-Lyapunov frame of Landa et al. 2012 is not implemented (Section 12), and no source treats mixed species in it, so every multi-ion result is in the pseudopotential frame. `Device.derived()` reports the error estimate max_i |u_{1,i}| / min_{ij} |r_i − r_j|, the fractional rf modulation of the Coulomb curvature (δK/K ≈ 3|u₁|/s): zero on the null, a note for a single ion **[derived]**.

### 4.2 Laser cooling and state preparation

The preparation module produces the run's initial motional and internal states. Each cooling stage has three levels of description sharing one parameter set: (A, the default) the rate framework of Section 4.2.2, in which each illuminated ion contributes phonon heating and cooling rates S(±ν_m) + 2D from its steady-state dipole-force spectrum and emission diffusion (Section 4.2.8), with its mass-weighted participation folded into the zero-point length, giving n̄_m = Σ heating/(Σ cooling − Σ heating) and the rate W_m; (B) the birth-death equation on Fock populations; (C) the master equation of the internal levels plus one mode, which validates A and B. The participation cancels from n̄ when every ion sees the same light and weights the rate. Level A raises `UncooledModeError` for a mode whose projection on every cooling beam is below (k̂·ê_m)² = 10⁻², whose illuminated ions barely take part (Σ_i c_{i,m}² < 10⁻⁴), or whose rate is too slow for a stated stage duration. A stage takes one atomic structure per ion, so a coolant and a qubit species share it.

#### 4.2.1 Doppler cooling

The semiclassical force model (RMP 2003 Eqs. 96-107) **[verified]**: F_a = ħkΓρ_ee with ρ_ee = (s/2)/[1 + s + (2(Δ − k·v)/Γ)²], which cools only for Δ < 0 and is minimal at Δ = −(Γ/2)√(1 + s) (Eq. 105's (1 + s) multiplies only Γ/(2Δ); Eq. 106's radical covers 1 + s alone) **[verified]**. With the RMP's ξ = 2/5 (Section 4.2.8), k_BT_min = 0.35ħΓ, 1.43 below Berkeland's ħΓ/2, so the two limits are not interchangeable **[corrected]**. The model needs ν ≪ Γ; at ν/Γ ~ 1 the floor scales as (Γ/2ν)² and the optimum detuning moves toward −ν. The Doppler stage is therefore the rate framework of Section 4.2.2 with W from the steady state of the ion's actual level scheme, valid at any ν/Γ **[corrected]**; at Δ = −Γ/2 and ν ≪ Γ it gives n̄_D = (Γ/4ν)(1 + α/cos²θ_L) − ½, the ½ being the zero-point offset **[recomputed here]**. The force model and the two-level closed forms are cross-checks for ν/Γ < 0.1 only. A beam has one detuning, saturation and direction for all 3N modes, so the stage moves each beam group's detuning to minimize the participation-weighted mean n̄ (weights b_{i,m}² of the next gate modes, uniform when none is scheduled) and reports every mode's residual; s and k̂ are inputs, since n̄ barely depends on s and the rate does **[corrected]**. One beam does not reproduce Monroe 1995's 9Be+ triple n̄ = (0.47, 0.30, 0.18) at any saturation (it needs the experiment's D1-D3 geometry), and neither the eight-level nor the two-level model reproduces Roos 2000's 40Ca+ n̄_z = 6.5(1.0), n̄_y = 16(2), the two agreeing within 10% (Section 9.3).

#### 4.2.2 Resolved-sideband cooling

**Single-photon scheme.** On the red sideband each cycle removes ħν and |0⟩ is dark: the rate out of |n⟩ is R_n = Γ̃(η√nΩ)²/[2(η√nΩ)² + Γ̃²] and ṗ₀ = p₁(ηΩ)²/Γ̃ − p₀[(Ω/2ν)²η̃²Γ̃ + (ηΩ/4ν)²Γ̃] (RMP 2003 Eqs. 110-116) **[verified]**, with Γ̃ the effective linewidth and η̃ the emitted photon's Lamb-Dicke parameter (Section 4.2.8). In a quenched scheme the recoil photon is the fast decay's: 393 nm for 40Ca+ cooled at 729 nm and quenched at 854 nm, (η̃/η)² = 1.37 **[verified]**. The floor is ⟨n⟩_min ≈ (Γ̃/2ν)²[(η̃/η)² + ¼] for γ ≪ ω_z and ω_z ≫ ω_R = η²ω_z **[verified]**. RMP Eq. 109 is printed non-Hermitian (iη(σ₊a − σ₋a†) is right) and Eq. 113 lacks a 1/(n̄ + 1) and a ½ in its sines **[corrected]**.

**The rate framework** (RMP Eqs. 117-121) **[verified]**:

R_{n→n+1} = (n+1) η² A₊,  R_{n→n−1} = n η² A₋,  A_± = W(Δ∓ν) + (η̃²/η²) W(Δ),  η̃²/η² = α k_em²/(Δk·ê_m)²,

with |e, n ± 1⟩ paired with Δ ∓ ν **[corrected]**, A_± bare rates in s⁻¹ and η² outside them. The carrier term is the recoil of carrier scattering; the participation multiplies η and η̃ alike and cancels, so the floor does not depend on N **[corrected]**, and for one beam along the mode the coefficients are Stenholm's A_± = P(Δ ∓ ν) + αP(Δ). The carrier term cancels in A₋ − A₊ but not in ⟨n⟩ = A₊/(A₋ − A₊) **[corrected]**. W is the readout rate object of Section 8.1, from one multi-level steady state; the two-level Lorentzian saturating at Γ/2 over-predicts the 171Yb+ detection rate by 2× against the Γ/4 ceiling **[corrected]**.

**Stimulated Raman** (Monroe et al. 1995; Wineland 1998) **[verified]**. Two beams detuned Δ from the P level drive |↓, n⟩ → |↑, n − 1⟩ at Ω₁Ω₂/(2Δ) in the convention of Section 4.3.1 (Monroe's g₁g₂/Δ, with g = Ω/2 and P = sin²(Ωτ), is half of it), and a repump returns the spin with Δn ≈ 0. The 9Be+ sequence is 50 µs of Doppler cooling (beams D1-D3), 7 µs of pumping into |2, 2⟩, then 1-3 µs red-sideband pulses and 7 µs repumps; five cycles per axis gave n̄ = (0.033, 0.022, 0.029) **[verified]**. Its η = (0.21, 0.12, 0.09) needs a 90° beam crossing, not counter-propagating beams, so |Δk| = 2k sin(θ/2) comes from the configured geometry **[corrected]**. A fixed pulse strands |↓, n⟩ where Ω_{n−1,n}t = 2mπ (mπ in the half-Rabi convention), which is why durations vary between cycles; the pulsed treatment is Section 4.2.8 **[verified]**. The preparation recipe's sideband stage is pulsed Raman cooling on a beam pair; single-photon cooling on a quadrupole line (40Ca+ at 729 nm) is not scheduled.

#### 4.2.3 EIT cooling

A strong blue-detuned coupling beam (Ω_r, Δ_r > 0) gives a weak cooling beam (Ω_g ≪ Ω_r) a Fano profile: a zero at Δ_g = Δ_r, which cancels carrier scattering, and a narrow resonance at the light shift of the ground-like dressed state, δ = (√(Δ_r² + Ω_r²) − |Δ_r|)/2 (Morigi, Eschner, Keitel 2000 Eq. 1) **[corrected]**; δ ≃ ν puts the red sideband on it **[verified]**. With Δ_r = Δ_g = Δ

A_± = (Ω_g²/γ) · γ²ν² / { γ²ν² + 4[Ω_r²/4 − ν(ν ∓ Δ)]² },

the upper sign heating **[verified]**, Ṗ(n) = η²[A₋((n+1)P(n+1) − nP(n)) + A₊(nP(n−1) − (n+1)P(n))] with η the projected difference of the two legs, zero in a Doppler-free geometry (Morigi 2003) **[verified]**, and

⟨n⟩_S = A₊/(A₋ − A₊) = [γ²ν² + 4(Ω_r²/4 − ν(ν+Δ))²] / [4Δν(Ω_r² − 4ν²)],

which is (γ/4Δ)² exactly at Ω_r = 2[ν(ν + Δ)]^{1/2} **[corrected]**; Δ ≤ 0 and Ω_r = 2ν raise `CoolingError`. At the RMP's tuning Ω_r² = 4νΔ_r the exact value is (Γ² + 4ν²)/(16Δ_r(Δ_r − ν)), not (Γ/4Δ_r)² **[corrected]**. Two coupling beams compose as Ω_r = (Σ_j Ω_j²)^{1/2}, and Morigi's Δ is the negative of this one **[corrected]**. There is no recoil floor: the recoil diffusion vanishes at two-photon resonance (Morigi 2003), so A_± has no carrier term **[verified]**; her Eq. 22 overstates the diffusion by 2 and her arXiv v1 Eq. 29 breaks probability conservation **[corrected]**. Level C is a four-level Zeeman-resolved master equation **[corrected]**; its floor lies above the three-level closed form (1.7× in Roos's geometry), because the π beam also drives S(−½) → P(−½). Section 9.3 carries Roos 2000 (40Ca+ to n̄_y = 0.18) and Lechner 2016 (18 ions to n̄ ≈ 0.01-0.02 at 19 × 10³ s⁻¹) **[verified]**; the mode nearer the bright resonance cools faster, R(3.29 MHz)/R(1.13 MHz) ≈ 3.2 at a 2.25 MHz light shift **[recomputed here]**.

#### 4.2.4 Polarization-gradient (Sisyphus) cooling

**The 171Yb+ scheme** (Ejtemaee and Haljan 2017) **[verified]**. Two counter-propagating cross-polarized beams at Δ/2π = +310 MHz on S₁/₂(F = 1) → P₁/₂(F = 0) in a 5.9 G transverse field cool all three axes (beam projections {½, ½, 1/√2}); the rate scales as s₀² **[corrected]**, single ions reach n̄ ≈ 1.5-2 at the optimum intensity, and the cooling is pulsed with a pump-plus-microwave reset against the dark |0, 0⟩ **[verified]**. Blue detuning is required on this and on any inverted j_e ≤ j_g line **[corrected]**. No source gives the scheme's analytic coefficients, so only its projections and the saturation bridge below are computed.

**The analytic model** (Joshi et al. 2020) **[verified]**. For j_g = ½ ↔ j_e = ½ driven lin-perp-lin along the quantization axis, U_± = ½mω_z²z² + (ħ/3)Δs ∓ (ħ/3)Δs sin(2kz + 2φ), with s the single-beam saturation referenced to the S₁/₂-P₃/₂ stretched line and φ the gradient phase at the centre; the z dependence belongs to the pumping rates only, since Eq. (1) already contains it (applying it twice halves the period) **[corrected]**. With ξ = Δs/(3ω_z) (from the D1 σ Rabi frequency, Ω₁ = √(2/3) Ω_stretched), the rates Γ_{±→∓} = (1/9)Γs(1 ∓ sin(2kz + 2φ)) and the recoil diffusion H_carr = (α/3)η²Γs(1 − sin²2φ), H_sb = (1/3)η²Γs(1 + sin²2φ) with the isotropic α = 1/3 **[recomputed here]**, linearization gives W(φ) = (16/9)η²Γsξ cos²2φ, H(φ) = (2/9)η²Γs(8ξ²cos⁴2φ + 2 + sin²2φ) and the steady state H/W − ½: ξ + 1/(4ξ) − ½ at φ = 0, and (3/4)ξ + 5/(8ξ) − ½ phase-averaged, minimal at √(15/8) − ½ = 0.8693 for ξ = √(5/6) **[recomputed here]**. The average holds for a gradient moving fast against W (W < δ < ω_z); for a static gradient each ion keeps its own H(φ)/W(φ) − ½, and the module averages over the ions' φ and raises when cos²2φ < 10⁻² **[corrected]**.

**The Lindblad layer.** The multi-level builder of Section 4.2.8 on the S₁/₂ and P₁/₂ Zeeman manifolds with one mode: two linear beams along B at phases (2φ, 0), so the circular standing waves have relative phase π/2; coupling amplitude √(1/3) = √(2/3) × 1/√2; the full exponential kẑ = η(a + a†) **[corrected]**. The dissipator has three recoil classes {−k, 0, +k} and nine operators, one per emitted polarization and class, with second moments 2/5 (σ) and 1/5 (π) **[recomputed here]**. Joshi's printed master equation lacks the −i/ħ, scales the jumps by Γ for √Γ and sums the recoil classes coherently, which inflates the decay by 2.8 for σ even as η → 0 **[corrected]**.

**Namespacing.** Joshi's s and Ejtemaee's s₀ = I/(51 mW/cm²) (on resonance, I_sat = πhcΓ/(3λ³) = 50.78 mW/cm² at 369.5 nm) are separate objects; the bridge s_Joshi = (3/2)s₀(Γ/2)²/((Γ/2)² + Δ²) is printed by neither paper **[background]**. The analytic prefactors hold for j_g = ½ ↔ j_e = ½ only, so no model validates another: 0.87 analytic, about 1 expected and 1.5-2 measured for 171Yb+, 7.7(1.1) outside the Lamb-Dicke regime in Joshi's experiment **[contested]**. The analytic model also drops the Zeeman term that sets the 171Yb+ CPT-free window (an 8.27 MHz splitting at 5.9 G) **[verified]**.

#### 4.2.5 Sympathetic cooling

A second species cooled with light the qubit does not see shares the energy through the normal modes. Home et al. 2009's 9Be+-24Mg+-24Mg+-9Be+ crystal, Doppler cooled on Mg+ to n̄ ≈ 15 and then sideband cooled on the second and first sidebands to ⟨n⟩ ≈ 0.06 per axial mode, is the archetype **[verified]**. It is modelled at level A (Section 4.2): the coolant is the illuminated set, shared modes take n̄ from its stage through their participation, the qubits' internal states are untouched, and a mode the coolant barely reaches is refused. Gates on mixed crystals are out of scope (Section 1.3), and the standard recipe is single-species (Section 4.2.6).

#### 4.2.6 Optical pumping and internal-state preparation

Optical pumping empties every level but the dark target, leaving an error set by off-resonant excitation of the target **[extracted]**. The order is Doppler cooling, then sideband or EIT cooling, then a final pump immediately before the circuit, with a re-pump after any recooling: Doppler cooling scrambles the internal state (171Yb+ over F = 1 through the 14.7 GHz sideband), so a pump done first is erased **[corrected]**. `PreparationSequence` refuses a sequence that does not start with Doppler cooling, a sub-Doppler stage without it, or a pump followed by cooling with no later pump; each mode takes n̄ from its last stage and each ion its state from its last pump. Pumping is simulated at level C, by the exact exponential of the multi-level Liouvillian (Section 4.2.8) from the scrambled state, which returns the preparation error (Section 8), the time, the photon count and the recoil heating per mode. 171Yb+ pumps to |F = 0, m = 0⟩ with a 2.1 GHz sideband on S₁/₂(F = 1) → P₁/₂(F = 1), 1/3 branching per excitation, and needs the 3.07 GHz sideband on the 935 nm repump **[extracted]**; 9Be+ pumps into |2, 2⟩ in 7 µs **[verified]**. The procedure is a device parameter, `Device.preparation` **[background]**: for a single-species crystal whose qubit's lower level is F = 0, `standard_recipe` builds detection light at −Γ/2 optimized over the gate modes, 10 second-order then 30 first-order Raman sideband pulses, and the pump on F = I + ½ → F′ = I + ½ at 0.5 I_sat; other qubits and mixed crystals carry an explicit recipe (the 40Ca+ optical preset's is 397 nm Doppler cooling with the 866 nm repump and a σ⁻ pump into m_J = −½).

#### 4.2.7 Thermometry and hand-off

After every stage the simulator can run the laboratory's diagnostics against the state it prepared: (i) sideband-ratio thermometry, P_e^{rsb}/P_e^{bsb} = [n̄/(n̄ + 1)]^k, exact in η, in the pulse duration and in the matrix element (Turchette 2000 Eqs. 8-11), whose constancy under varying pulse time tests thermality **[verified]**; (ii) blue-sideband flopping, P_↓(τ) = ½(1 + Σ_n P_n e^{−γ_nτ} cos(2Ω_{n+1,n}τ)) (Wineland 1998 Eq. 42, flopping at 2Ω), inverted for P_n by non-negative least squares **[verified]**; (iii) the carrier's Debye-Waller reduction over every coupled mode, Ω̄ = Ω^{(0)} Π_p Σ_n P_n e^{−η_p²/2} L_n(η_p²) = Ω^{(0)} Π_p exp[−η_p²(n̄_p + ½)], an identity for thermal states (Wineland 1998 Eqs. 122-124), which the carrier calibration of Section 7.5 fits **[verified]**; (iv) after pulsed cooling, where the state is not thermal, Rasmusson's time-averaged red-sideband signal ½Σ_{n≥m} p(n), which stepping m inverts for the populations, and a double-thermal fit **[verified]**. The hand-off is a product of per-mode states, n̄ or the explicit level-B or level-C density matrix passed on unchanged, with the internal state of Section 4.2.6; level A agrees with level C where both apply (Section 9.3). A pulsed-sideband-cooled mode is handed off as the thermal state of its n̄, and its preparation note compares ⟨n²⟩ and the 10⁻⁴ tail level with the pulsed distribution (default 171Yb+ chain: 7.3× and 5.6×, n = 20 and 15 against 2) **[recomputed here]**.

#### 4.2.8 Spontaneous emission with recoil, and the multi-level master equation

This subsection specifies the emission-recoil kernel, which sets η̃ (Section 4.2.2), the angular factor of the Doppler limit (Section 4.2.1), the recoil of repump photons and of scattering during gates (Section 6.5), and the multi-level master equation it belongs to **[background]**.

**The recoil term.** A photon of wavevector k_eg emitted along k̂ displaces the motion by e^{−ik_eg k̂·x̂}; averaged over the pattern N_q(k̂) of polarization type q,

𝓛_se ρ = Σ_{e→g, q} Γ_{e→g,q} [ ∫ dΩ_k N_q(k̂) e^{−ik_eg k̂·x̂} |g⟩⟨e| ρ |e⟩⟨g| e^{+ik_eg k̂·x̂} − ½{|e⟩⟨e|, ρ} ],

with N₀ = (3/8π) sin²θ for π decays and N_{±1} = (3/16π)(1 + cos²θ) for σ decays, θ measured from B̂. With k̂·x̂_i = Σ_m b_{i,m}(k̂·ê_m) x_{0,m}(a_m + a_m†), the kick is Π_m D_m(−ik_eg b_{i,m} x_{0,m} k̂·ê_m), built exactly (Section 5.1.1), one set per emitting ion and channel, with the participation inside **[corrected]**. Pattern normalizations are not mixed: Itano's and Steck's density integrates to one over the sphere, Steck's reduced f̃(u) to one on [−1, 1], and Cirac 1992's W(x) carries the measure dx/2, so f̃ = W/2; Cirac's W = (3/4)(1 + x²) is the σ pattern along the axis, not a generic dipole pattern **[corrected]**.

**Angular factors.** To second order the pattern enters through α_m = ∫ dΩ N_q (k̂·ê_m)²: a photon from ion i heats mode m by α_m b_{i,m}²(k_eg x_{0,m})² quanta, so η̃_{i,m}² = α_m b_{i,m}²(k_eg x_{0,m})², and one axis's modes together receive the free-ion recoil (ħk)²/2m_i times its α **[recomputed here]**. A π decay has α = 1/5 along B and 2/5 across, a σ decay 2/5 along and 3/10 across, isotropic emission 1/3 **[recomputed here]**. This is Stenholm 1986's α (Eqs. 4.16-4.24), which weights the emitted photon only, the absorbed one supplying the 1 of his (1 + α); his Eqs. 4.31b-c are misprinted, his Eqs. 4.34-4.38 right **[corrected]**. His A_± = P(Δ ∓ ν) + αP(Δ) gives ⟨n⟩ = (γ/ν)²(α + ¼), γ the half-width, the RMP floor with (η̃/η)² = α **[verified]**. Wineland and Itano 1979 give the same exact kernel (their Eq. 31 is the Laguerre element of Section 4.3.1), k_BT_min = ħγ/2 for a free atom and the moments ⟨ΔE⟩ = R, ⟨(ΔE)²⟩ − R² = (2/3)R⟨H_t⟩ **[verified]**; their bookkeeping charges 2R per event to the driven axis (α = 1), and their isotropic split (4R/3 along, R/3 across) is replaced by the trace-one tensor, R(1 + α_∥) on the driven axis and Rα_⊥ on the others **[corrected]**. The RMP's ξ = 2/5 is thus the σ pattern along B, and any comparison of Doppler limits states its α. A 171Yb+ photon at 369.5 nm on a 3 MHz mode (kx₀ = 0.053) deposits 1.1 × 10⁻³ quanta, so a pump's few photons leave Δn ≈ 3 × 10⁻³ and Doppler cooling's 10⁴ photons tens of quanta.

**Rate coefficients** **[verified]**. (i) The emission diffusion is D = ½ Σ_{i→j} ρ_ii^{SS} Γ_{i→j} α_{ij,m} η_ij², with each channel's own wavenumber and angular factor; with Cirac's half-rate dissipator 2D𝒟[X], X = a + a†, the phonon heating rate is 2D, which equals αη_em²R_sc only for a single pattern and wavenumber (one mean α is up to 37% wrong for mixed π and σ channels) **[corrected]**. Cirac's γ_ji is a half rate; Marzoli's Eq. 4 reads as full rates, twice this, and his Eq. B4 confirms the half rate **[corrected]**. α multiplies only the emitted photon: the absorbed photon's e^{ik_L·x̂} is in the Hamiltonian and never in a collapse operator, and the V-system D = (s₁₀/4)ρ₀₀[Γ₁₂(η₁₀² + αη₁₂²) + Γ₁₀η₁₀²(1 + α)] tests both rules. (ii) The phonon rates are η²A_± = S(±ν) + 2D, with S(ω) = ∫ e^{−iωτ}⟨δF(τ)δF(0)⟩ dτ the dipole-force spectrum of the internal steady state (QuTiP's sign; at weak drive S(ω) = η²W(Δ − ω)), exact in the saturation; at weak drive A_± = (Ω²/Γ)[L(Δ ∓ ν) + (η̃²/η²)L(Δ)] with L = Γ²/(4Δ² + Γ²) (Eschner et al. 2003 Eq. 6 with cos²θ_L inside η and α inside (η̃/η)²). The saturated W in the W(Δ ∓ ν) form is off by exactly 1 + s, so that form is refused above Ω/Γ = 0.1; Cirac's Eqs. 50 and 52 flip the sideband sign and lack a factor 2η² **[corrected]**. (iii) η² = ω_R/ν cancels from n̄, so recoil sets the rate and not the floor: at Δ = −ν, Γ ≪ ν, n̄_SB = (Γ/2ν)²[α(k_em/k_L)²/cos²θ_L + ¼] (Roos 2000 Eq. 3.20), and α → 0 leaves (Γ/4ν)² **[verified]**. In the Doppler regime Itano and Wineland's three-beam optimum shares the friction as γ_si/γ_tot = √f_si/Σ_j √f_sj, and a beam along one axis leaves the other axes without a steady state (`UncooledModeError`) **[verified]**. (iv) A quenched or repumped level has Γ′ = ΠΓ₁₀ and γ′ = Π(Γ₁₀ + Γ₁₂)/2 with Π = s_aux/2 (Marzoli Eqs. 12, 15; Roos Eq. 3.21), so γ′/Γ′ = (Γ₁₀ + Γ₁₂)/(2Γ₁₀), resolved sidebands need ν ≫ Γ′ and ν ≫ γ′, and the V configuration cools at δ₂₀ = +ν **[verified]**; Cirac's Λ system with a repumper reaches ⟨n⟩_min = 1/√β − ½ at Ω_φ² = 4Δν/√β **[verified]**. Printed errors not to copy **[corrected]**: Cirac Eq. 7 (the operator order, which would weight diffusion by the lower level), Eqs. 28 and 54; Itano and Wineland Eq. 14; Steck Eqs. 5.522 (the pattern weight omitted), 19.212 and 19.214; Roos Eq. 3.11 (the Laguerre index n for n_< when m < 0).

**Validity.** A closed form or rate model raises `ValidityError` outside its regime unless the caller passes its escape keyword; "≪" is a ratio below 0.1: Ω/Γ (`allow_saturation`); η²(2n̄ + 1) < 0.25 on every mode, emitted photon included (`allow_strong_coupling`); W against ν and against every internal rate (no escape); Γ > ω_R for Doppler cooling (`allow_recoil_limited`). Two gaps the sources leave are filled by construction **[background]**: the joint multi-mode kick is one sampled direction applied to every mode (tested by Σ_axes α = 1 and Itano's Σ_i⟨ΔE_i⟩ = 2R + ħk·v), and an oblique mode axis or a q-coherent decay uses the vector jump operator Σ_q(ε̂*_{k̂λ}·ê_q) d_q e^{−ik_em k̂·x̂}, from which α is derived rather than hard-coded.

**Collapse operators.** Each channel's pattern is discretized on K weighted directions (Gauss-Legendre in cos θ times trapezoid in φ, or Lebedev) into operators √(Γw_k) |g⟩⟨e| ⊗ Π_m D_m(−ik_eg b_{i,m} x_{0,m} k̂_k·ê_m), entered incoherently. For a mode on a principal axis the marginals are (3/4)(1 − u²) (π along, α = 1/5), (3/8)(1 + u²) (σ along or π across, 2/5), (9 − 3u²)/16 (σ across, 3/10) and ½ (isotropic); two Gauss-Legendre nodes return α = 1/3 for every quadratic marginal, so three are the minimum for the second moment, and six to eight suffice for the Δn kernel at η_em ≤ 0.9 **[corrected]**. The Lamb-Dicke default is the minimal set exact in the first two moments, a ±k̂ pair of weight α/2 per axis with the rest on a no-recoil operator (six directions about B in three dimensions); the full quadrature is the option for hot ions. Three assertions precede any solve: Σ_j p_j = 1 and Σ_j p_ju_j² = α to 10⁻¹²; Σ_k C_k†C_k = Γ|e⟩⟨e| ⊗ 𝟙 (the kicks are unitary); and one kick from |e, 0⟩ gives ⟨n_m⟩ = α_m b_{i,m}² η_em² with ⟨p⟩ = 0. `mcsolve` draws discrete kicks and `mesolve` integrates the averaged kernel; the semi-analytic path takes the internal steady state and S(±ν), and raises `CoolingError` when A₋ ≤ A₊. Without recoil the operators are the internal-state collapse operators of Section 4.5.5, the d > 2 model of Section 6.5.

**The multi-level optical Bloch equations.** Cooling, pumping and detection share one builder over the levels of Section 4.5: H = Σ_p E_p|p⟩⟨p| + Σ_beams Σ_{(p,q)} (ħ/2) Ω^{(beam)}_{pq}(t) e^{−iω_beam t} |q⟩⟨p| + h.c., with the field-dependent energies of Section 4.5.1 and the polarization-resolved Rabi frequencies of Section 4.5.2, plus a mode and its displacement operators at level C. Frame frequencies are assigned manifold by manifold along the beams, a level reached only by decay taking the transition frequency; a beam inconsistent with an assigned manifold (two tones on one line, a loop of beams that does not close) leaves a beat, and on internal-only spaces the steady state is then the period-averaged fixed point of the one-period propagator (integrated with `vern9`), with propagation over 1000/Γ as the fallback for incommensurate beats **[background]**. Dissipation is one operator per decay line and emitted polarization, [ω³/(3πε₀ħc³)]^{1/2} times that line's block of the dipole operator, so Σ_q C_q†C_q = Γ_{U→L}P_U and excited-state coherence feeds ground coherence; never one operator per excited sublevel (Berkeland 1998 Eq. 12 as printed) **[corrected]**. Decay out of the modelled levels follows a leak policy: include (the default), sink (a bookkeeping state) or renormalize. The steady state is the single scattering-rate object of Section 13: W(Δ) for cooling, R_∘, R_d and R_b for readout (Section 8.1) and coherent population trapping, every photon rate checked against the equal-population ceiling n_e/(n_e + n_g) (Γ/4 for the 171Yb+ F = 1 → F′ = 0 cycle); its evolution gives the pumping of Section 4.2.6, and with one mode it is the level-C cooling of Sections 4.2.1 to 4.2.4. With several closed sets of states (no beam or decay leaves them) the steady state is the one reached from the lowest level unpolarized, and the report names the sets. Each effective two-level form (Γ̃, the EIT profile, the Doppler force) follows by adiabatic elimination (Marzoli et al. 1994) and is tested against level C in its regime.

**Pulsed sideband cooling** (Che et al. 2017; Rasmusson et al. 2021) **[verified]**. A k-th-order red-sideband pulse of duration t followed by pumping is a column-stochastic matrix W_k(t) on the Fock populations, with diagonal cos²(Ω_{n,n−k}t/2) and k-th upper diagonal sin²(Ω_{n,n−k}t/2), Ω_{n,n−k} = Ω₀|⟨n − k|D(iη)|n⟩| (Section 4.3.1); pulses compose as an ordered product. The schedules are one shared duration, independent durations, or one duration per order with higher orders first (Che's 75 pulses for 25Mg+ at η = 0.3), and a coarse grid precedes the bounded search because n̄ oscillates in t. The element ∝ L^{(k)}_{n−k}(η²) has zeros, so single-order cooling strands population at round(n′_zero + k), n′_zero the first zero as a continuous degree (n = 41 and 74 for k = 1 and 2 at η = 0.3) **[recomputed here]**; Che's Fig. 2 labels the degree n′ = n − k (40 and 72), his Eq. 4 drops the thermal exponent n, and his carrier Ω₀e^{−η²/2} holds at n = 0 only **[corrected]**. A repump is the recoil kernel Σ_k Poisson(k) K^k with the photon-weighted α of its own emitted channels, and its off-resonant Raman heating is Δn̄ = t_re s_re Γ_re³η²/(4Δ_HF²) with angular Γ and Δ_HF (0.03 quanta per 10 µs repump for 25Mg+) **[recomputed here]**. Post-cooling thermometry is Section 4.2.7.

### 4.3 Spin-motion interaction Hamiltonian and single-qubit control

Every pulse (a calibration scan, a gate, a cooling pulse) is a set of drive terms on a shared space, assembled by one Hamiltonian builder. This section fixes those terms; Section 13 lists the conventions the sources use.

#### 4.3.1 The Hamiltonian the builder implements

After the optical rotating-wave approximation (dropping e^{±i(ω_L+ω₀)t}) the drive of ion i is, exactly in η (Monroe 2020 Eqs. 6-8; RMP 2003 Eqs. 60-63; Wineland 2003 Eq. 2.1) **[verified]**,

H = Σ_i (Ω_i/2) σ₊^i e^{i(δk X̄_i − μ_i t − δφ_i)} Π_m exp[iη_{im}(a_m† e^{iω_m t} + a_m e^{−iω_m t})] + h.c.,

with μ_i = ω_L − ω₀ the detuning from the carrier and η_{im} the Lamb-Dicke parameter (Section 4.1.7). The builder keeps the motion in the Schrödinger picture (Section 5.2): a drive is one time-independent operator σ₊^i ⊗ Π_m D_m(iη_{im}) (Section 5.1.1) with a scalar coefficient (Ω(t)/2) e^{−i(μt − φ(t))} per tone. Ω is derived from the beams and the dipole matrix elements (Sections 4.3.2, 4.5.4), never typed in, and the same coefficient carries crosstalk (Section 6.6), excess micromotion (Section 4.3.6), beam curvature (Section 6.2) and sampled laser noise (Sections 6.3, 6.4). Resonances sit at μ_i = Σ_m ℓ_m ω_m; the sideband of total order p carries η^p/√(p!), since ⟨p|D(iη)|0⟩ = e^{−η²/2}(iη)^p/√(p!) **[corrected]**. RMP 2003 Eqs. 67-68 drop a factor ½ against its own Eq. 37 (ν = β_x ω_rf/2) **[corrected]**.

**Matrix elements.** ⟨n′|D(iη)|n⟩ = e^{−η²/2} (iη)^{|n′−n|} (n_<!/n_>!)^{1/2} L^{|n′−n|}_{n_<}(η²) (Wineland 1998 Eq. 18; RMP Eq. 70) **[verified]**. The builder uses the signed element; a Rabi frequency is its modulus, Ω_{n′,n} = Ω|⟨n′|D(iη)|n⟩| **[corrected]**. In the Lamb-Dicke regime (η√⟨(a+a†)²⟩ ≪ 1, a condition on the state) the carrier, red and blue sidebands flop at Ω, ηΩ√n and ηΩ√(n+1), the red a Jaynes-Cummings and the blue an anti-Jaynes-Cummings coupling (RMP Eq. 72) **[verified]**.
